Augustine read it as *caritas*, charity: the foolish virgins had the form of devotion but not the abiding love that sustains it. Whatever the precise referent, the parable's logic is clear: **readiness cannot be transferred.** When the lamps begin to fail, the wise refuse to share — "lest there should not be enough for us and you." Grace, the Spirit, and a formed character are personal realities. One cannot borrow another person's faith at the last hour; one must have been growing in it all along.

**The shut door.** The climax is stark. The bridegroom comes; those who were ready go in with him, "and the door was shut." The latecomers' plea — "Lord, Lord, open to us" — sounds like genuine prayer, yet the answer is, "I do not know you." This echoes Luke 13:25. The horror of the scene is that these women were *in the wedding party* and still lost their place. The lesson is not that God is cruel but that the hour of decision has a finality: there comes a time when profession must be proven by possession, when "Lord, Lord" is answered with silence.

**"Watch therefore."** The application (v. 13) is the key to the whole chapter: *you do not know the day or the hour.* Watchfulness here is not anxious vigilance but a settled hope that orders daily life — living each day as if it might be the last, because for any one of us it may be.

---

#### II. The Parable of the Talents (25:14–30)

**The stakes.** A *talent* was not a small coin but an enormous sum — roughly six thousand denarii, about twenty years' wages for a common laborer. Five talents were a fortune. The master's entrusting his goods to his servants before a long journey is a picture of God giving believers real responsibility in the world during the absence of Christ.

**"According to his ability."** The distribution — five, two, one — is made "to each according to his own ability." This is grace meeting people where they are: God does not give everyone the same measure, and he will judge each by what was entrusted, not by a uniform standard. The first two servants respond *immediately* — "he went and traded" — which is itself significant. Faith acts promptly on trust in the master's character.

**The one-talent servant.** His defense is the theological heart of the parable: "I knew you as a harsh man, reaping where you did not sow." He has reduced God to an accountant, a taskmaster to be managed and minimized. His sin was not cowardice alone but a *corrupt theology* — fear instead of trust. So he does the one thing that is safe for himself: he buries the money and returns it untouched. But in doing so he has betrayed the very relationship that made him a steward. The master's reply is devastating in its fairness: even the timid minimum — depositing the money with bankers — would have produced something. **Doing nothing out of fear is not safety; it is unfaithfulness.**

**The principle of grace and growth.** "To everyone who has, more will be given, and he will have abundance; but from the one who has not, even what he has will be taken away" (v. 29). This is not a doctrine of salvation by works — these servants were already inside the household, already entrusted. It is the law of spiritual life: grace used grows; grace hoarded atrophies. The faithful servants are rewarded not with wages but with relationship and responsibility: "Enter into the joy of your master." The contrast with the "outer darkness, where there will be weeping and gnashing of teeth" (cf. 8:12) shows that unfaithfulness within the household has real and serious consequences.

---

#### III. The Parable of the Sheep and the Goats (25:31–46)

**From parable to vision.** Here Jesus steps out of figurative speech into plain apocalyptic language: "When the Son of Man comes in his glory, and all the holy angels with him, then he will sit on the throne of his glory." This is no longer a household illustration but the final judgment of *all the nations* — the widest scope in the Gospel. The shepherd imagery draws on Psalm 23, Ezekiel 34, and Zechariah 10:3. (In practice a shepherd separates goats from sheep first; the reversal here underscores that the sheep's place at the right hand is entirely by grace.)

**The criterion of judgment.** What distinguishes the two groups is not creed, ritual, or church membership but six acts of mercy toward the hungry, thirsty, stranger, naked, sick, and imprisoned — the basic needs of human life. And both groups are astonished. The righteous ask, "When did we see you?" Their surprise reveals that they served *without keeping score*, without calculating merit before God. They acted from love, not performance. The judgment is therefore not a ledger of works but the revelation of what a heart's orientation has actually produced: those who loved Christ's people (and their neighbors) loved him; those who passed by the suffering in indifference had, in effect, refused him.

**"The least of these my brothers."** This is one of the most Christological statements in the Gospels. Jesus identifies himself with the suffering — "as you did it to one of the least of these, you did it to me." In the earliest church, "my brothers" likely included persecuted fellow believers; but the text's logic extends beyond any group: wherever a human being is broken by hunger, exile, nakedness, sickness, or imprisonment, Christ meets us there. To serve the least is to serve the King; to neglect them is to neglect him.

**The two destinies.** The fire "prepared for the devil and his angels" (v. 41) indicates that the fate of those who reject mercy is not some afterthought but their chosen alignment with the powers of evil. The chapter's final words — "These will go away into eternal punishment, but the righteous into eternal life" — close Matthew's record of Jesus' teaching on the two ways, echoing the Sermon on the Mount (7:13–14) and framing everything between.

---

#### Conclusion: What It Means to Wait for Christ

The three parables form a deliberate movement **inward, stewardship, outward**: be ready in your own soul; be faithful with what God has given you; be merciful to the people around you. None of them permits a Christianity of mere expectation. The delay of the Parousia is not an excuse for dormancy but the very space in which character is formed — oil gathered, talents multiplied, mercy practiced.

And there is a final irony worth meditating: this chapter on watchfulness and faithfulness ends just before the account of Christ's own arrest, trial, and death. The one who commands "watch therefore" will himself be found sleeping no more in Gethsemane, faithful to the end, entering his joy through the cross. For the Christian, waiting for the King is ultimately modeled by the King — who waited, suffered, and was faithful so that his people might enter, at last, into his joy.

### Chapter 26

#### Introduction: The Hour Arrives

Matthew 26 is the hinge chapter of the Gospel. Everything that has gone before—the kingdom proclamations, the miracles, the controversies, the Olivet discourse—converges here into a single week in which Jesus walks deliberately toward the cross. The chapter's dominant note is not confusion but *sovereignty*. From the first verse, Jesus speaks of his death as something he himself will be "delivered up" to accomplish (v. 2). He is no passive victim of Jewish or Roman politics; he is the obedient Son who sets the terms of his own passion. The whole chapter moves in ironies: the plot of men becomes the instrument of God's salvation; anointing for burial and betrayal share the same table; Peter's boldest words precede his deepest failure. And through it all, Scripture is being fulfilled "that it might be so" (v. 54).

#### I. The Prediction and the Plot (vv. 1–5)

Jesus announces plainly: "after two days the Passover is coming, and the Son of Man will be delivered up to be crucified." Note the deliberate pairing of Passover with crucifixion. Matthew wants his readers to see that Jesus' death is not an accident of Roman occupation but the fulfillment of Israel's redemption story. The language "will be delivered up" (paradothēsetai) is passive in form but active in meaning—Jesus hands himself over, as John will later make explicit: "No one takes it from me, but I lay it down of my own accord" (John 10:18).

Meanwhile, Caiaphas and the Sanhedrin plot to seize Jesus "by deceit." Their caution—"not during the feast, lest a riot occur"—is both realistic and ironic. They fear a popular uprising over a man whose entry into Jerusalem was met with hosannas. Yet their timing fails them: God's hour, not theirs, governs the events. The chapter thus opens with two councils in tension—the council of salvation in the heart of Christ, and the council of death in the court of Caiaphas—and only one of them will prevail.

#### II. The Anointing at Bethany (vv. 6–13)

In the house of Simon the leper (healed, as John 12 identifies him, by Jesus), a woman pours very expensive ointment on Jesus' head. The disciples' indignation—"Why this waste? For this ointment might have been sold for much and given to the poor"—is not wrong in principle; care for the poor is a constant theme of Scripture (cf. Lev. 19:9–10). But it misses the moment. Jesus' reply contains two truths that correct them:

1. **"You always have the poor with you, but you don't always have me."** His earthly presence is drawing to its close. The window for such an act of worship is narrow.
2. **"She did it to prepare me for burial."** The woman's act was prophetic. In a culture where anointing heads signified honor and anointing bodies signified burial, she has done both at once—honoring the Messiah while anticipating his death. She understood what the disciples, who had followed him for years, did not: that this man was going to die.

Jesus' promise that "wherever this Good News is preached in the whole world, what this woman has done will also be spoken of as a memorial of her" shows how the church's memory preserves acts of costly devotion. Her broken alabaster jar (as Mark adds) is a picture of total, uncalculated love—precisely the posture of worship.

#### III. The Bargain of Judas (vv. 14–16)

Immediately after this scene of supreme devotion comes its opposite: "What are you willing to give me if I deliver him to you?" Thirty pieces of silver—the price of a slave in Exodus 21:32, and the sum later associated with Zechariah's "price set on him by the sons of Israel" (Zech. 11:12–13). The tragedy is not only that Judas sells his Master, but that he does so for so little, and that "from that time he sought opportunity to betray him"—the betrayal was premeditated, a settled purpose rather than a moment of weakness. The contrast with the woman in Bethany could not be sharper: one gives everything without being asked; the other prices his friend's blood at slave value.

#### IV. The Passover and the Lord's Supper (vv. 17–25)

The disciples' question—"Where do you want us to prepare... the Passover?"—is answered by Jesus with a word of foreknowledge that leaves no detail to chance: "My time is at hand." Even the location of his final meal is in his hands.

At the table, two things happen that reshape Christian worship forever.

**The prediction of betrayal (vv. 21–25).** "One of you will betray me" turns the most intimate meal in Jewish life—the Passover, eaten with family and friends—into a scene of shattering intimacy. The betrayer is one who has "dipped his hand with me in the dish," sharing the common bowl. Jesus' words carry both grief and judgment: "Woe to that man through whom the Son of Man is betrayed! It would be better for that man if he had not been born." Yet note what follows: Jesus does not eject Judas from the table. He continues the meal with him present—a quiet witness to the reach of grace, however it is received.

**The institution of the Supper (vv. 26–28).** Taking bread, giving thanks, breaking it, and saying "Take, eat; this is my body," Jesus reinterprets the Passover from within. The unleavened bread that had pointed to Israel's hasty redemption from Egypt now points to his body, which will be broken for their deliverance. The cup—"my blood of the new covenant, poured out for many for the remission of sins"—quotes Jeremiah 31:31–34 and Isaiah 52:15. The old covenant was ratified with the blood of animals (Exod. 24:8); the new covenant is ratified with his own blood. "For many" is atoning language: this blood is poured out *for* others, to accomplish what no sacrifice of the old order could—remission of sins.

And then the forward look: "I will not drink of this fruit of the vine... until that day when I drink it anew with you in my Father's Kingdom." The Supper is thus an eschatological meal. It looks back to the cross, looks forward to the messianic banquet at Christ's return, and holds both together in the present act of remembrance. For the Christian church, this table is where the new covenant is proclaimed, the body of Christ is confessed, and hope for the Kingdom is rehearsed until he comes.

#### V. The Prediction of Falling Away (vv. 26–32)

After the hymn (the Passover Hallel), on the way to the Mount of Olives, Jesus quotes Zechariah 13:7: "I will strike the shepherd, and the sheep of the flock will be scattered." But he immediately adds a word no prophet had added: **"But after I am raised up, I will go before you into Galilee."** The scattering is real; it is not final. Resurrection precedes restoration. This verse is the seed of the entire post-resurrection Gospel.

Peter's response—"Even if all stumble because of you, I will never stumble"—is the natural voice of discipleship: sincere, brave, and blind to its own weakness. Jesus' answer is specific and sorrowful: "Tonight, before the rooster crows, you will deny me three times." Peter escalates—death itself would be easier than denial—and all the others echo him. The chapter thus sets up a pattern that runs through Christian experience: the best intentions of the flesh are no substitute for the grace of God.

#### VI. Gethsemane (vv. 33–46)

This is perhaps the most human passage in the Gospels, and one of the most theologically profound. "My soul is exceedingly sorrowful, even to death" (v. 38)—the Greek intensifies it: a deep grief has seized his soul. The Son of God experiences anguish to the point of death, not because he doubts the Father, but because the "cup" he must drink is the wrath of God against sin, which he bears in our place.

His prayer is the model of all Christian prayer:

- **It is honest:** "If it is possible, let this cup pass from me." He does not perform stoicism; he brings his real human horror before the Father.
- **It is submissive:** "Nevertheless, not what I desire, but what you desire." The second and third prayers strip away even the conditional—pure surrender: "Your will be done."
- **It is repeated:** Three times, with increasing intensity, showing that prayer in the hour of trial is a battle, not a formula.

And his rebuke of the sleeping disciples gives us one of the great summaries of the Christian life: **"Watch and pray, that you don't enter into temptation. The spirit indeed is willing, but the flesh is weak."** Peter's failure at Gethsemane (sleep) and in the courtyard (denial) are one failure in two acts. The remedy Jesus names is not greater self-discipline but watchfulness joined to prayer—dependence on God rather than on our own resolve.

When he returns the third time, the tone shifts: "The hour is at hand, and the Son of Man is betrayed into the hands of sinners. Arise, let's be going." The agony ends; the obedience begins. He rises not because the sorrow has passed but because the Father's will has been embraced.

#### VII. The Arrest (vv. 47–56)

Judas arrives with "a great multitude with swords and clubs," armed to take a man who has refused all earthly resistance. His sign—the kiss—perverts the most affectionate gesture into an instrument of capture. And Jesus' reply, **"Friend, why are you here?"** (or "Friend, do what you came for"), is staggering in its grace. Even at the moment of betrayal, he addresses Judas as *talis*—friend. There is no reviling, no self-defense, no attempt to escape.

When one of the disciples (Peter, according to John 18) draws his sword and cuts off the high priest's servant's ear, Jesus commands: "Put your sword back into its place, for all those who take the sword will die by the sword." He then asks a question that is both an answer and a mystery: could he not call on his Father for "more than twelve legions of angels" (over seventy thousand soldiers)? The power was his; he simply chose not to use it. "How then would the Scriptures be fulfilled that it must be so?" The Servant's way—suffering without violence (Isa. 53:7)—must be walked to the end.

His words to the arresting crowd are a quiet indictment: "Have you come out as against a robber...? I sat daily in the temple teaching, and you didn't arrest me." He was never hidden; they simply refused to see. And with that, "all the disciples left him and fled"—Zechariah 13:7 fulfilled to the letter. The scattered sheep will be gathered again, but not until after the shepherd is struck.

#### VIII. The Trial Before Caiaphas (vv. 57–68)

The night trial before the Sanhedrin is a scene of grotesque injustice. They "sought false testimony" and could not find any that would stick—because Jesus had told the truth, and truth cannot be falsified into a capital charge. At last two witnesses offer a garbled version of his temple words: "I am able to destroy the temple of God, and to build it in three days."

Jesus' first response is silence—"as a man who does not open his mouth" (Isa. 53:7). Then Caiaphas presses with an oath of extraordinary weight: "I adjure you by the living God, tell us whether you are the Christ, the Son of God." And Jesus answers: **"You have said so"**—an echo of Peter's confession at Caesarea Philippi (16:16), now returned to the high priest who rejected it. But he does not stop at a title. He adds the two great Old Testament pictures of the Messiah's destiny: Daniel 7:13 ("coming on the clouds of heaven") and Psalm 110:1 ("sitting at the right hand of Power"). The one they are trying as a criminal is the coming Judge, enthroned in glory.

Caiaphas tears his robes—the ancient sign of extreme outrage—and declares blasphemy. In the Jewish understanding, to claim the divine throne and the clouds of heaven was to make oneself God; if he was not God, it was death-worthy presumption; if he was God, the charge proves his innocence. The Sanhedrin's verdict—"He is worthy of death!"—is thus built on a confession they cannot use. Their mockery ("Prophesy to us, you Christ! Who hit you?") is the final humiliation before the final hour.

#### IX. Peter's Denial (vv. 69–75)

While Jesus is being broken in the inner court, Peter is being broken in the outer one. The three denials escalate with the pressure: a casual denial to a servant girl; a denial "with an oath" on the porch; and finally a denial "cursing and swearing." The bystanders' observation is devastating in its simplicity: "Your speech makes you known"—his Galilean accent, the one thing he could not control, betrays him.

But the chapter does not end with Peter's failure. "Immediately the rooster crowed. Peter remembered the word which Jesus had said to him... Then he went out and wept bitterly." That weeping is everything. It is the death of the self-reliant Peter and the beginning of the restored one. The bitter tears in the courtyard are the same grace that will later produce the Peter who preaches at Pentecost, who feeds the sheep on the shore (John 21:15–17), and who writes, "Blessed be the God and Father of our Lord Jesus Christ, who according to his great mercy has caused us to be born again... through the resurrection of Jesus Christ from the dead" (1 Pet. 1:3). The promise of v. 32—"after I am raised up, I will go before you into Galilee"—is where Peter's story resumes.

#### Conclusion: Themes for the Christian Life

Matthew 26 gathers together nearly everything the church needs to understand about itself and its Lord:

1. **Christ's sovereignty over his own death.** He is delivered up by his own hand, in his own time, for his own purpose. The cross is not tragedy overcome but victory accomplished.
2. **The Passover fulfilled in him.** "Christ our Passover lamb has been sacrificed" (1 Cor. 5:7). At the Lord's Supper we eat and drink not merely a memorial but a participation—his body, his blood, the new covenant, the remission of sins, and the hope of drinking again with him in the Father's Kingdom.
3. **The school of Gethsemane.** In our own hours of trial, we are invited to pray as he prayed: honestly, submissively, persistently—and to watch and pray so that we do not enter into temptation, knowing the flesh is weak.
4. **Grace after failure.** Judas' despair and Peter's tears end in opposite places. The difference is repentance. There is no Christian life without falling; there is a Christian life of rising again, because the risen Lord goes before us into Galilee.
5. **The irony of redemption.** The very instruments of Christ's death—deceit, silver, a kiss, false witnesses, a sword, a denial—are all held within the hand of the Father and made to serve the fulfillment of Scripture. "All this has happened that the Scriptures of the prophets might be fulfilled" (v. 56).

The chapter ends in a courtyard at night, with a weeping man and a condemned Messiah. But the reader who knows what follows—empty tomb, risen Lord, scattered sheep gathered—reads these verses not as an ending but as the darkest hour before the dawn. The shepherd is struck; the flock is scattered; and then, "after I am raised up," everything begins again.

### Chapter 27

#### Introduction

Matthew 27 is the darkest chapter in the Gospels and, paradoxically, one of the most glorious. It records the hours between the condemnation in the high priest's court and the sealing of the tomb — the Passion itself. Read through the lens of the whole Gospel, this chapter shows that the cross was not a tragedy that overtook Jesus but the very destination toward which he walked: "Behold, we go up to Jerusalem... and the Son of Man will be delivered" (20:18). Everything in this chapter — the silence, the mockery, the darkness, the torn veil — is the Lamb carrying out his office. As the early church sang from Psalm 22, which Jesus himself quoted on the cross, "They pierced my hands and my feet... they divided my garments among them" (vv. 16–18). The psalmist's words, written centuries earlier, are fulfilled in this chapter almost line by line.

---

#### I. The Final Council and Delivery to Pilate (vv. 1–2)

"When morning came," the chief priests and elders finalize what they had begun in the night. Matthew compresses; Mark and Luke fill in the nighttime trials. What matters here is the character of the scene: the religious leaders, who styled themselves guardians of the Law, conspire to kill an innocent man — and they must borrow Roman power to do it, for under occupation they could not execute. The irony is deliberate. Those who accused Jesus of blasphemy are themselves guilty of the greater crime, and their "counsel" against him is the counsel of unbelief that runs through the whole chapter.

Note also what Matthew does *not* record: no defense speech, no counter-accusation. Jesus is bound and led away like a criminal. This is the portrait of Isaiah 53: "He was oppressed, and he was afflicted, yet he opened not his mouth; like a lamb that is brought to the slaughter" (Isa. 53:7). His silence is not weakness but obedience — the Father's plan being accomplished in the Son's willing submission.

#### II. Judas: Remorse Without Repentance (vv. 3–10)

Judas "saw that Jesus was condemned" and was seized with remorse (*metamelēthē*). But observe carefully what his remorse produces: he returns the money, but he does not return to Christ. He confesses sin — "I have sinned in betraying innocent blood" — yet he makes no appeal to mercy, offers no prayer, seeks no forgiveness. His grief is the grief of a man who has concluded that his sin is unforgivable, and so he hangs himself. The distinction the church has always drawn is confirmed here: remorse without faith is despair; repentance is turning back to God. Judas had the first without the second.

The priests' reply — "What is that to us? See to it yourself" (v. 4) — is one of the coldest lines in Scripture. The money, "the price of blood," is too defiled for the temple treasury, so they buy a potter's field outside the city for burying strangers. Two fulfillments are at work:

1. **The prophecy.** Matthew cites "Jeremiah," though the words come from Zechariah 11:12–13. The ancient and modern explanations converge on this: Jeremiah may be named as the representative of the prophetic corpus (as "Moses" stands for the Pentateuch), or Matthew may have had in mind a lost work of Jeremiah, or the error may be scribal. What matters is that the event fulfills prophecy — the price set on the Son of God by "some of the children of Israel" becomes the purchase-money of a field of blood.
2. **The irony.** The money paid to betray the Messiah ends up buying ground for burying *strangers* — foreigners, Gentiles. There is a quiet prophetic echo here of the gospel's ultimate destination: the price of Christ's betrayal will, in the end, serve the burial and the hope of all nations.

#### III. Jesus Before Pilate (vv. 11–26)

**"Are you the King of the Jews?"** This is the question that frames the trial, and Jesus' answer — "You say so" (*sy iei*) — is neither a full claim nor a denial. He accepts the title on their terms: yes, in the sense you mean, though your sense is wrong. His kingdom is not of this world (John 18:36); his throne will be a cross. When the accusations pile up and he answers nothing, "the governor marveled greatly" (v. 14). Pilate, a practical man who has heard every defense in the empire, recognizes something unprecedented in the silence of an innocent man. Isaiah's dumb lamb before the shears is on trial, and the Roman governor senses it.

**Barabbas.** The feast custom of releasing a prisoner sets up the chapter's great moral inversion: the crowd that shouted "Hosanna" a week earlier now demands the release of a notorious criminal — Barabbas, whose name means "son of the father," an ironic counter-name to the Son of God. Pilate knows they handed Jesus over out of envy (v. 18), and even his wife sends a warning from a dream: "Have nothing to do with that righteous man" (v. 19). God's witness comes through every channel — a Roman governor, an unnamed Gentile wife — and all are ignored.

**The washing of the hands.** Pilate's gesture echoes Deuteronomy 21:6–8, the ritual washing of hands over an uninvestigated murder. But water does not wash off guilt. By yielding to the mob to preserve his position, Pilate makes himself a party to the crime; history and theology have never excused him. And the people's answer — "His blood be on us and on our children!" (v. 25) — is a self-imposed curse. A word of pastoral caution is needed: this verse has been horribly misused through the centuries as a warrant for hatred of the Jewish people. The guilt it names belongs to those who spoke it, and to every person in every age who rejects Christ — Jew and Gentile alike (cf. Acts 4:25–27, where David's psalm is applied to "the rulers... gathered against your holy servant Jesus"). It is a warning, not a weapon.

Pilate releases Barabbas and hands Jesus over to be flogged — the flagellation that would leave the victim half-dead before the cross was ever reached.

#### IV. The Mockery of the Soldiers (vv. 27–31)

The soldiers' parody is the chapter's most piercing scene, for it is a parody that accidentally tells the truth. They dress him in scarlet — royal purple — braid a crown of thorns, place a reed as a scepter, and kneel: "Hail, King of the Jews!" In their cruelty they perform an act of worship before their true King. The thorn crown is the crown of a fallen world; the reed, the scepter of a kingdom that rules by suffering. And when they spit on him and strike his head, Isaiah 50:6 is fulfilled to the letter: "I gave my back to those who struck me, and my cheeks to those who pulled out the beard; I did not hide my face from shame and spitting."

The church has always seen in this mockery a strange liturgy: the first "adoration of the Kings" performed by Roman soldiers. What they meant as insult is, in God's economy, an acclamation.

#### V. The Crucifixion at Golgotha (vv. 32–38)

**Simon of Cyrene** is pressed into service to carry the cross — a detail that shows how the Passion drew even unwilling bystanders into its orbit (tradition holds that his son Alexander later appears in the church, Mark 15:21). **Golgotha**, "the place of a skull," was a hill outside the city, a place of execution. The fathers saw in it a deeper echo: Adam, formed from the dust of the ground, died; at the place of skulls the new Adam is put to death, that in him all might be raised (1 Cor. 15:45–47).

The **sour wine mixed with gall** offered before the crucifixion fulfills Psalm 69:21 — "they gave me also gall for my meat; and in my thirst they gave me vinegar to drink." Jesus tastes it and refuses: he will not dilute the cup. He will drink it to the dregs, as the vinegar on the sponge at v. 48 shows.

The **dividing of garments by lot** fulfills Psalm 22:18 exactly. The **inscription** — "THIS IS JESUS, THE KING OF THE JEWS" — is set over his head as a public joke; John records that Pilate refused to alter it even when the priests objected (John 19:19–20). It is the signboard of the whole chapter and of the age: the King of the Jews reigns from a cross, and his title will be proclaimed to every nation. The **two robbers** crucified with him fulfill Isaiah 53:12 — "he was numbered with the transgressors" — the righteous one placed among the guilty, which is the very mechanism of substitution.

#### VI. Threefold Mockery (vv. 39–43)

The taunts come in three waves, and each assumes that messiahship means power:

- **The passersby**: "If you are the Son of God, come down from the cross!"
- **The chief priests, scribes, Pharisees, and elders**: "He saved others; he cannot save himself... Let God deliver him now if he desires him."
- **The robbers**: the same reproach.

The logic of the taunt is the logic of the world: a God who cannot save himself from a cross is no God at all. The answer, of course, is that he *is* saving — by staying on it. The cross redefines power as self-giving (Mark 10:45), and "coming down" would be the one thing that would forfeit everything. In Luke's account one of the robbers turns from mockery to faith ("Remember me, Lord"); Matthew records only the reproach here, but the pattern is the same: at the foot of the cross, unbelief and faith stand side by side.

#### VII. Darkness, Dereliction, Death (vv. 44–50)

**"From the sixth hour there was darkness over all the land until the ninth"** — noon to three in the afternoon, a darkness that swallowed the whole region. This is not an eclipse but a theophany of judgment: the sun itself refuses to shine on the place where the sin of the world is being concentrated and borne. It is the night of Gethsemane extended over the land, and it is the shadow of death falling on the Son.

Then the cry: **"Eli, Eli, lema sabachthani?"** — "My God, my God, why have you forsaken me?" (v. 44). Matthew gives us the words in Aramaic, the language Jesus actually spoke, and then translates them into Psalm 22:1. This is not the whimper of a man who has lost his faith; it is the quotation of a psalm that begins in abandonment and ends in vindication — "For God has not forsaken or disdained the afflicted of the poor man" (Ps. 22:24). In this cry Jesus identifies himself with the suffering righteous one who represents Israel, and he bears what no human being should bear: the actual experience of separation from the Father under the weight of sin. The Father does not abandon the Son; the Son *bears* abandonment so that we never have to. It is the heart of the atonement in a single sentence.

The bystanders misunderstand him as calling for **Elijah** — the prophet expected to come before the Messiah (Mal. 4:5) — and one runs with a sponge of vinegar on a reed, while the others say, "Let it be; let us see whether Elijah will come to deliver him" (v. 49). Salvation is literally offered at the foot of the cross, and they decline it. The tragedy of the chapter is complete: God's own Son dies within arm's reach of his witnesses, and they watch.

**"Jesus cried out again with a loud voice and yielded up his spirit"** (v. 50). The Greek (*paradidōmi to pneuma*) means "he handed over his spirit." This is not the gasp of a man dying of exhaustion; it is the voluntary surrender of life by one who said, "I lay down my life... no one takes it from me, but I lay it down of myself" (John 10:17–18). The Lamb chooses the moment.

#### VIII. The Veil Torn and the Centurion's Confession (vv. 51–54)

The signs that follow are not theatrical; they are theological:

- **The veil of the temple was torn in two from top to bottom.** From *top to bottom* — by God's hand, not man's. The great curtain separating the Holy of Holies is ripped open at the moment of death: the way into the presence of God no longer runs through a building, a priesthood, or a calendar, but through the torn flesh of Christ himself (Heb. 10:19–20). "Behold" (*idou*) — Matthew's word of revelation — marks this as the chapter's central sign.
- **The earth quaked and the rocks were split.** Creation itself convulses at the death of its Creator; the hard rock, like the heart of stone (Ezek. 36:26), is broken open.
- **The tombs were opened, and many bodies of the saints... were raised**, appearing in the holy city after his resurrection. This is a uniquely Matthean sign — a foretaste of the general resurrection breaking into history, death's storehouse thrown open by the power that will soon empty the tomb itself.

And then the confession: **"Truly this was the Son of God!"** (v. 54). It comes from the centurion and his men — Gentile soldiers standing outside the temple precincts. This is the first unambiguous confession of Christ's divinity in Matthew, and it echoes Peter's confession at Caesarea Philippi (16:16) while answering it: what Peter confessed by faith on a hillside, the centurion confesses by sight at the foot of the cross. The gospel to the Gentiles begins not in a synagogue but in the terror of a Roman officer.

#### IX. The Faithful Women and Joseph of Arimathea (vv. 55–61)

**"Many women were there watching from afar"** — Mary Magdalene, Mary the mother of James and Joses, and the mother of Zebedee's sons. When the disciples had fled, the women stayed. They served him in Galilee; they stand with him at the end. Their faithfulness here is not incidental: these same women will be the first witnesses of the empty tomb (28:1). The resurrection will be announced first to those who refused to look away from the cross.

**Joseph of Arimathea**, a rich man, a disciple of Jesus — and, Mark adds, a member of the council that condemned him (Mark 15:43; John 19:38 calls him a "secret" disciple for fear of the Jews). At the one moment when discipleship cost everything, he comes forward openly. He asks Pilate for the body, wraps it in clean linen, and lays it in **his own new tomb, cut in the rock** — a tomb in which no one had ever yet been laid. The newness matters: this tomb is prepared for a resurrection, not merely a burial. The great stone is rolled against the door — not to keep Jesus in, but to secure the body and seal the scene.

#### X. The Sealed Tomb (vv. 62–66)

The chapter closes with its most ironic verse. The very men who killed Jesus return to Pilate, remembering his prediction: "After three days I will rise again." They ask for a guard, lest the disciples steal the body and stage "the last deception... worse than the first." Pilate's reply is laconic: "You have a guard; make it as secure as you can" (v. 65).

The irony is total. Their enemies believed him more than they did. They seal the stone, set the watch, bind every human possibility — and God raises him anyway. The guard they posted to prevent the resurrection becomes, in the next chapter, its involuntary witness. Every measure taken against the gospel is swallowed by it. The chapter ends in darkness, death, and a sealed tomb — but the seed of Easter is already planted in that stone.

---

#### Conclusion: What the Cross Teaches Us

Matthew 27 gathers into a single day the whole theology of the atonement. In it we see:

1. **Substitution.** The innocent one stands in the place of the guilty; he is numbered with transgressors, cursed that we might be blessed (Gal. 3:13).
2. **Silence as strength.** Christ's dumbness before his accusers is not defeat but the obedience by which "many shall be made righteous" (Isa. 53:11).
3. **The fulfillment of Scripture.** From Psalm 22 to Zechariah 11, the prophets' words land on this day like arrows — the cross was foretold, therefore it was intended, therefore it was certain.
4. **A way opened.** The torn veil means that "boldness" into God's presence is now available to every believer (Heb. 10:19). No mediator but the Mediator; no curtain but the cross.
5. **The certainty of resurrection.** The sealed tomb, the posted guard, the remembered prediction — all point forward to the morning when an angel rolls away the stone and a woman runs to tell the disciples.

And for us, the chapter asks its own questions, as the crowd's did: *Which will you choose — Barabbas or Jesus?* Can we wash our hands of him and call ourselves innocent? Will we pass by wagging our heads, or will we stand with the women from afar, serving, watching, refusing to look away? The centurion's confession is still the only adequate response to what happened on that hill: *Truly this was the Son of God.* And because he died — and because the stone was rolled away by a hand no guard could stop — we may add what the centurion did not yet know: *and he lives.*

### Chapter 28

#### Introduction: The Fulfillment of All Things

Matthew's Gospel has been building toward this chapter from its first page. In 1:22–23 the child Jesus is named *Emmanuel*, "God with us," and the Gospel closes in 28:20 with the risen Lord's promise, "I am with you always." The three predictions of his death and resurrection (16:21; 17:22–23; 20:18–19) have now come to pass. When the angel says, "He has risen, **just as he said**" (v. 6), we hear the echo of Matthew's signature fulfillment formula that has run through the whole book. The story of Israel's Messiah, told through genealogy, birth, ministry, and passion, reaches its climax: the tomb is empty, and the crucified one is Lord.

The chapter divides naturally into three movements: the discovery of the empty tomb (vv. 1–10), the false report of the guards (vv. 11–15), and the Great Commission on the mountain in Galilee (vv. 16–20).

#### I. The Tomb at Dawn (vv. 1–10)

**"Now after the Sabbath, as it began to dawn on the first day of the week"** (v. 1). Matthew's phrasing deliberately recalls Genesis 1:5 ("And there was evening and there was morning..."). A new creation is dawning. The "first day of the week" becomes for the church the Lord's Day, the weekly memorial of the resurrection — the day on which Christians have gathered in worship ever since.

**The women as first witnesses.** Mary Magdalene and "the other Mary" are the same faithful women who watched the burial (27:61). While the male disciples fled from Gethsemane (26:40–41) and hid behind locked doors, these women keep their vigil. In first-century Jewish culture a woman's testimony was not admitted in court; yet the earliest resurrection accounts name women as the first witnesses of the risen Lord. The honest conclusion is that this detail was too embarrassing to invent — it testifies to the historicity of what happened. God's resurrection overturns human hierarchies: the first heralds of the gospel are those the world had ranked lowest.

**The earthquake and the angel.** Matthew records earthquakes at moments of divine intervention (27:51, at the moment of Jesus' death). Now the earth shakes again as "an angel of the Lord descended from heaven." His appearance — "like lightning," his garments "white as snow" — evokes the Sinai theophany and the vision of Daniel 7, where the Ancient of Days is clothed in white. The stone is rolled away not so that Jesus can exit, but so that the tomb can be seen to be open: the angel *sits* on it, a sentinel over the empty grave. There is an echo here of Isaiah 26:16, "O Lord, when you were in distress we called, and you delivered us... roll away the stone from above us." The stone that sealed death's door has been rolled aside by heaven itself.

**"He is not here, for he has risen"** (v. 6). This is the hinge of the Christian faith. Paul will later say that if Christ has not been raised, "your faith is futile" (1 Cor. 15:17). The empty tomb, however, is not itself the message — the angel does not point to the vacancy but to the Person: "Come, see the place where the Lord was lying." Faith in Matthew is never detached from history; it is an invitation to look, to verify, and then to go and tell.

**The command to Galilee.** The risen Jesus will be seen "in Galilee" — the region where his public ministry began (4:12–17) and where the disciples had failed so memorably: the storm on the sea, Peter sinking beneath the waves (14:24–31). The place of their weakness becomes the place of encounter. Grace returns to the site of failure.

**"Fear and great joy"** (v. 8). Easter is paradoxical: the women leave "with fear and great joy." Fear is not abolished but transformed — it remains, as it did in the angel's presence, the awe of creatures standing before the holy God, now mingled with the joy of redemption.

**"Rejoice!"** (v. 9). The Greek *chairete* became for two thousand years the church's Easter greeting, still exchanged in Orthodox and Catholic liturgy. The women "took hold of his feet and worshiped him" — a striking detail. The risen body is real, tangible, touchable; this is no phantom. And Jesus' response, "Do not be afraid," is one of Matthew's recurring refrains, now spoken by the risen Lord himself to those who have come to worship. Note also his word: **"my brothers."** The scattered, failing disciples are reconstituted as a family around the risen Christ — and so are we, who hear this gospel.

#### II. The False Report (vv. 11–15)

While the women run with the good news, "some of the guards" go into the city and report to the chief priests. The religious leaders, assembled with the elders in council, do what power does when it cannot refute the truth: it buys a counter-narrative. A large sum of silver is offered on one condition — *say* that the disciples stole the body while you slept.

Matthew's inclusion of this passage is itself a clue to his purpose. He writes with full knowledge that this rumor was already circulating: "This saying was spread abroad among the Jews, and continues until today" (v. 15). He does not answer it with argument but with narrative, and the narrative answers for itself. Consider its absurdity: these were Roman soldiers whose duty it was to guard a prisoner; if they had truly slept on watch, no amount of silver could have saved them from the governor's judgment — yet the priests promise only to "persuade" Pilate. More pointedly, the very story they are paid to tell is a confession that the tomb was empty. The stolen-body theory concedes everything and proves nothing.

There is also a bitter irony in the money itself. Judas received thirty pieces of silver for betraying Jesus (27:3–10); now the chief priests pay a far larger sum to bury the truth of his resurrection. Both payments end in exposure. The passage stands as a standing warning about what happens when religious authority meets the gospel: it will try to roll another stone across the door. And the warning is not confined to first-century Jerusalem — every age produces its bribes, its distractions, and its "they slept" explanations for the scandal of the resurrection.

#### III. The Mountain and the Commission (vv. 16–20)

**The mountain in Galilee.** "The eleven disciples went into Galilee, to the mountain where Jesus had appointed them." Mountains are where heaven touches earth in Matthew's Gospel: Jesus teaches from a mountain (chs. 5–7), he is transfigured on a mountain (ch. 17), and now he commissions his church from a mountain. This is a new Sinai — but where the old covenant was given to one nation under threat, the new commission is given to all nations under promise.

**"They worshiped him; but some doubted"** (v. 17). Matthew's honesty here is one of the Gospel's most precious gifts. The disciples' response is not uniform certainty; worship and doubt stand side by side in the same breath. Faith, as Matthew portrays it, is not the absence of questions but the presence of the risen Lord to whom questions are brought. Tellingly, the text does not exclude the doubters from what follows — they too hear the commission. (A few early manuscripts omit "but some doubted," but the weight of evidence preserves it, and the church has always found comfort in its inclusion.)

**"All authority has been given to me in heaven and on earth"** (v. 18). This is the theological foundation of the Great Commission, and it echoes Daniel 7:13–14, where "one like a son of man" is given "dominion, glory, and a kingdom." The man who was mocked as "King of the Jews" (27:37) now possesses every authority in cosmos. The command that follows — *go* — is not a suggestion issued by a defeated martyr's memory; it is an order from the Lord of heaven and earth. We go because he has all authority, and because his authority is now exercised through his people.

**"Make disciples of all nations"** (v. 19). The phrase *panta ta ethnē* — "all the nations," all the peoples — bursts open the story. Matthew's Gospel had seemed to narrow: Jesus told his disciples not to go to the Gentiles but to "the lost sheep of the house of Israel" (10:5–6). But that was a stage in the unfolding, not the end. The promise made to Abraham — that in his seed "all the families of the earth shall be blessed" (Gen. 12:3; 22:18) — is now fulfilled in Christ. The church is by definition a cross-cultural people.

**"Baptizing them in the name of the Father and of the Son and of the Holy Spirit."** This is the New Testament's great baptismal formula, and one of its most explicit Trinitarian statements: one God, revealed in three persons, into whose unified life the disciple is immersed. Baptism here marks entry into discipleship — a public dying and rising with Christ (cf. Rom. 6:3–4; 1 Cor. 12:13), incorporation into the community of the new covenant.

**"Teaching them to observe all that I have commanded you."** The risen Jesus, the new Moses, hands over his teaching as the rule of life for his people. This is why Matthew's Gospel is structured around great blocks of Jesus' instruction — above all the Sermon on the Mount. Discipleship in Matthew is not merely believing *about* Jesus but being formed by his words, "all that I have commanded you."

**"Behold, I am with you always, to the end of the age"** (v. 20). The promise that closes the Gospel is the promise that opened it: *Emmanuel*, God with us. The language echoes Isaiah's comfort for a people in exile — "Fear not, for I am with you" (Isa. 41:10; 43:2). And "the end of the age" (*synteleia tou aiōnos*) is Matthew's own eschatological phrase (13:40, 49; 24:3): the Lord's presence spans all of history, from the resurrection to the consummation. No mission, no persecution, no ordinary Tuesday morning falls outside the reach of his company. The church goes into a world it cannot control, but it does not go alone.

#### Conclusion: What This Chapter Gives the Church

Matthew 28 is at once a report, a rebuke, and a commission. It is a **report** — history happened: on the first day of the week the tomb was found empty, and the crucified Jesus was encountered alive by his followers, beginning with the women. It is a **rebuke** — to every generation of chief priests who would rather buy a story than kneel before the truth. And it is a **commission** — to make disciples of all nations, baptizing and teaching them, under the authority of the risen Lord.

For the believer, the chapter's deepest gift is its final word. The Gospel that began with "God with us" ends with "I am with you always." Between those two promises stands the whole of Christian life: worship like the women at the tomb, obedience like the eleven on the mountain, honesty about our doubts, and the unshakeable assurance that the one who rolled away the stone has not left his church to face the night. The stone is rolled. The door is open. The Lord is risen — *chairete* — and he goes before us still.

## Mark

### Chapter 1

#### Introduction: The Gospel as an *Aretalogia* of the Servant-King

Mark opens without a genealogy, without a nativity narrative, without any delay. In the first verse he names his subject and his thesis: "The beginning of the Good News (*euangelion*) of Jesus Christ, the Son of God." The word *beginning* (Greek *arche*) echoes Genesis 1:1 ("In the beginning…") and, more pointedly, the Septuagint rendering of Isaiah 52:7. Mark is announcing a new creation: the story of God's good message is, in its deepest sense, the story of what God is doing in and through the person of Jesus Christ, the Son of God.

Notice the frame. The title "Son of God" (v. 1) will be echoed and deepened by the voice from heaven at the baptism (v. 11), by the confession of the unclean spirit (v. 24), and by Peter in a later chapter. This is the "Messianic Secret" at work from the outset—Mark's signature device. He reveals Christ's identity, yet withholds full public confession until after the cross. The reader is invited to see what the characters in the story slowly come (or fail) to see.

The chapter divides naturally into the opening of the story and the first cluster of wonders:

1. **John the Baptist prepares the way** (1–8)
2. **The baptism and anointing of Jesus** (9–11)
3. **The temptation in the wilderness** (12–13)
4. **The Galilean proclamation** (14–15)
5. **The calling of the first disciples** (16–20)
6. **Authority in the synagogue: the unclean spirit** (21–28)
7. **Healing Simon's mother-in-law and the evening crowds** (29–34)
8. **Prayer and the itinerant mission** (35–39)
9. **The cleansing of the leper** (40–45)

---

#### I. John the Baptist: The Forerunner of the Covenant (vv. 1–8)

Mark quotes Isaiah 40:3 and Malachi 3:1, the two great "messenger" texts. The composite citation is deliberate: it places John squarely within the prophetic tradition of God preparing the way for a coming act of salvation. The wilderness voice—crying, "Make ready the way of the Lord! Make his paths straight!"—carries the double image of a road through rough terrain and of a crooked people being made straight before God.

John's ministry is twofold: *baptism of repentance for the forgiveness of sins* (v. 4). The crowd's response is striking—"all the country of Judea and all those of Jerusalem"—an outpouring that anticipates the Pentecost crowds. They went out to the Jordan, confessed their sins, and were immersed. Repentance (*metanoia*), in Mark, is not a mere feeling of remorse but a changed orientation of life; it is the posture required of all who would enter the story of the Kingdom.

John's appearance—camel's hair, a leather belt, locusts and wild honey—identifies him as a prophet in the lineage of Elijah (2 Kings 1:8). More than a fashion statement, it is a sign of radical simplicity and separation from the comforts of the day. His self-abasement is the chapter's most striking confessional note: "After me comes he who is mightier than I, the thong of whose sandals I am not worthy to stoop down and loosen" (v. 7). In the ancient world the slave untied the master's sandals. John claims the lowest office before Jesus. And he draws the line that will govern the whole chapter and the whole Gospel: "I baptized you in water, but *he* will baptize you in the Holy Spirit" (v. 8). John points beyond himself; his ministry is the anteroom to the Messiah's.

*Application:* The Christian life rightly begins where John stands—confessing, repenting, and pointing to the One who is mightier. A healthy witness is one that continually redirects attention from itself to Christ.

---

#### II. The Baptism and Heavenly Sonship (vv. 9–11)

"In those days" (v. 9) is Mark's way of marking a pivot in salvation history. Jesus, "from Nazareth of Galilee," comes to the Jordan and is baptized by John. For a Christian reader the phrase "was baptized" is theologically rich: by being submerged with sinners, the sinless One identifies with the very people whose repentance he has come to redeem. He takes his place in the line of Israel's confession.

The threefold manifestation in v. 10–11 is the heart of the chapter:

- **The heavens are torn open** ("parting"). The Greek verb *schizo* means to rend, as cloth. Mark, more than any other evangelist, insists on the *rending* of heaven (cf. the temple veil in 15:38). The barrier between God and humanity is not gradually lowered but violently opened. What was sealed is now split, and the divine presence pours into history.
- **The Spirit descends like a dove.** This recalls the Spirit hovering over the waters in Genesis 1:2 and is, in Christian reading, the first clear presentation of the triune God: the Son in the water, the Spirit in descent, the Father in voice. The dove (a gentle, unaggressive bird) signals the Spirit not as an overwhelming force but as a presence that rests upon and empowers.
- **The Father's voice:** "You are my beloved Son, in whom I am well pleased." This blends Psalm 2:7 ("You are my Son") with Isaiah 42:1 (the Servant "in whom I am well pleased"). Mark thus fuses *king* and *servant* in a single sentence: the Messiah is both the anointed Son and the suffering Servant.

The baptism is, in Christian tradition, the *theophany*—the self-revelation of God—where the identity of Jesus that the whole Gospel will unfold is declared by heaven itself. It is also the New Testament counterpart to Israel's crossing of the Jordan into the land (Joshua 3–4): the people enter the promised land, and now, with Christ, a new people enter a new inheritance.

*Application:* The baptismal scene is the model for Christian baptism and for the ongoing life of the believer: a death-and-resurrection with Christ, the indwelling of the Spirit, and the Father's delight in us "in Christ."

---

#### III. The Wilderness: Israel's Story Re-enacted (vv. 12–13)

The very next verse—"Immediately the Spirit *drove* him out into the wilderness" (v. 12)—uses a violent verb. The Spirit does not gently suggest; He propels Jesus into testing. Forty days in the wilderness, "tempted by Satan," places Jesus in deliberate parallel with Israel, whom God tested for forty years in the desert (Deuteronomy 8:2–3; 9:21; Psalm 95:8). Where Israel failed, the Son will obey.

Mark tells the temptation in a single sentence (Matthew and Luke expand it into three specific tests), yet the theology is dense: the wilderness, the adversary, the wild animals, and the serving angels. The wild beasts evoke Isaiah 11:6–9 (the peaceful kingdom) and, in the LXX, the "wild asses" of Isaiah 13:21, a picture of desolation. Either way, they mark Jesus as the one who will reign over creation. The angels *serving* him (v. 13) is the decisive note: the true Son is the one *served by angels*, which is precisely what Satan offers to dethrone him as (see 14:61–62). In brief, the wilderness proves, in obedience, what the baptism declared in word: Jesus is the true Israel and the true Son.

*Application:* The Christian is invited into the same pattern—spirit-led testing that confirms identity through obedience rather than through comfort.

---

#### IV. The Kerygma: Kingdom, Repentance, Faith (vv. 14–15)

A crucial historical hinge: "Now after John was *taken into custody*" (v. 14). Mark does not flinch. The Baptist is imprisoned, and it is in that shadow—after the forerunner has been silenced—that Jesus begins his public ministry in Galilee. The movement of the story is from Judea to Galilee, from the Baptist's voice to the Master's word.

The announcement is the compact creed of the chapter, and one might call it the "programmatic statement" of Mark's Gospel:

> "The time is fulfilled, and God's Kingdom is at hand! Repent, and believe in the Good News." (v. 15)

- **The time is fulfilled** (*peplerotai ho kairos*): the appointed, ripe moment of promise has arrived.
- **The Kingdom of God is at hand** (*ephugen*—has drawn near): the reign God had promised through the prophets is breaking into history.
- **Repent and believe**: the required human response is the same double movement John inaugurated—a turning of life and a trust in the message.

This is the *euangelion*—Good News—not because the words are pleasant but because they announce that God himself is coming to reign. Everything that follows in the Gospel (words, wonders, conflict, death, resurrection) hangs on this verse.

---

#### V. The Calling of the First Disciples (vv. 16–20)

By the Sea of Galilee Jesus meets four fishermen: Simon (Peter) and Andrew, then James and John, sons of Zebedee. The call is startling in its directness: "Come after me, and I will make you into fishers of *men* (or *people*)" (v. 17). There is no résumé, no interview, no training program—only the authority of the calling word. And the response is Mark's characteristic *immediately*: "Immediately they left their nets and followed him." The sons of Zebedee go a step further and leave their father and their hired workers (v. 20).

The image is rich. Fishermen become *fishers of men*: the labor of casting and hauling is transferred to the gathering of God's people. The cost is immediate and total—nets, boats, livelihood, family. Yet Mark does not romanticize; he will later show Peter's failure, denial, and restoration. The calling is real, and it is costly, but it is the beginning of a new vocation in which the ordinary (fishing) is transfigured into the mission of the Kingdom.

*Application:* Discipleship in Mark is *immediate, costly, and trusting*—a "follow me" that requires us to let the Master redefine our purpose.

---

#### VI. Authority in the Synagogue: The Unclean Spirit (vv. 21–28)

They go to Capernaum and, "immediately on the Sabbath," Jesus enters the synagogue and *teaches* (v. 21). The crowd is "astonished," for "he taught them *as having authority* (exousia), and not as the scribes" (v. 22). The scribes taught by appeal to tradition and to other teachers; Jesus teaches from a place of authority as if from the One who speaks. This is the first of several "wonder" (*dynamis*) stories in Mark, and it establishes a pattern: **word → wonder → wonder at the word.**

The man with the unclean spirit interrupts. The demon's confession is the first in a series of correct identifications of Jesus: "I know who you are: the *Holy One of God!*" (v. 24). The demon calls Jesus "the Nazarene" (a term of contempt) and asks a chilling question: "Have you come to *destroy* us?" The question exposes the real issue: Jesus is not merely a rabbi; He is the Lord whose coming means the end of the demon's realm.

Jesus' command is short and sovereign: "Be quiet, and come out of him!" (v. 25). Mark notes the *convulsing* and the *loud voice* as the demon departs—the violence of the conflict hidden beneath the miracle. The crowd's amazement returns (v. 27): "A new teaching? For with authority he commands even the unclean spirits, and they obey him!" The "report… went out immediately everywhere into all the region of Galilee" (v. 28)—the first of several waves of spreading fame that will keep drawing crowds to Jesus.

*Application:* The Christian proclamation carries authority not by human eloquence but by the presence of the risen Christ. The Gospel is not a suggestion; it is the word of the One before whom even the powers of darkness must be "quiet."

---

#### VII. Healing and the Evening Crowds (vv. 29–34)

Leaving the synagogue, they enter the house of Simon and Andrew, where Simon's mother-in-law "lay sick with a fever" (v. 30). Jesus "took her by the hand and raised her up" (v. 31). The physical touch is striking, especially in the context of an unclean spirit just minutes before—Jesus moves from the impure demon to the fevered woman and is defiled by neither. He *raises her up* and the fever leaves; the detail that "she *served* them" (v. 31) is a gentle echo of the Servant-Son theme: healed, she returns to service.

"At evening, when the sun had set" (v. 32)—a careful nod to the Sabbath's rest (the healing of the demon in the synagogue had already occurred on the Sabbath, but Mark here marks the end of the day)—the whole city brings "all who were sick and those who possessed by demons" to the door. He "healed many" and "cast out many demons." The restraint is telling: "He *didn't allow the demons to speak, because they knew him*" (v. 34). The demons' confessions, though true, would make a spectacle of the identity that Jesus wants to reveal in his own way and at his own hour.

*Application:* The Gospel meets both the *disease* and the *demon*—the physical and the spiritual—because the wholeness Jesus offers is total. And there is wisdom in restraint: not every truth needs to be shouted before its time.

---

#### VIII. Prayer and the Itinerant Mission (vv. 35–39)

One of the most quietly powerful pictures in the Gospel: "Early in the morning, while it was still dark, he rose up and went out, and departed into a deserted place, and *prayed there*" (v. 35). The one who commands demons, who is called "Holy One of God," rises before dawn to pray. The disciples find him and report the crowd: "Everyone is looking for you." His answer reorients the whole enterprise: "Let's go elsewhere into the next towns, that *I may preach there also, because I came out for this reason*" (v. 38).

This verse is a master-key to understanding Mark. Jesus' identity is not sustained by wonder-working but by *preaching*; his ministry is not a response to demand but to *commission*. "Because I came out for this reason" grounds his entire life in the Father's sending. Prayer is the fuel of proclamation: the man who cast out a demon and healed a city rises to pray before he preaches.

*Application:* The Christian worker is tempted to live by applause and activity. Jesus models the priority that saves us—the deserted place, the dark morning, the prayer that precedes the preaching. The mission is not "for this reason" we are popular; it is for the preaching that we came.

---

#### IX. The Cleansing of the Leper (vv. 40–45)

The chapter closes with a leper who "came to him, begging him, *kneeling down* to him, and saying, 'If you want to, you can make me clean'" (v. 40). Leprosy made a person ceremonially unclean and socially exiled. This man's approach—kneeling, pleading—is the posture of total dependence, yet his words carry a sober, beautiful faith: *if you will, you can.* He does not presume; he trusts in Jesus' power and submits to Jesus' will.

The response is moving: "Being *moved with compassion* (splanchnistheis—literally, 'moved in the entrails,' a deep gut-level pity), he stretched out his hand, and *touched him*" (v. 41). To touch a leper would normally make the toucher unclean. Jesus does the opposite of the religious reflex: He reaches toward the outcast. "I want to. Be made clean." The healing is immediate; the leprosy departs.

Then a command full of practical and theological wisdom: "See that you say nothing to anybody, but go show yourself to the priest and offer for your cleansing the things which Moses commanded, for a *testimony* to them" (v. 44). The silence (the "Messianic Secret" again) protects the mystery; the priestly examination honors the Law; the Levitical offering (Leviticus 14) is offered "as a testimony"—so that the priests themselves would have undeniable proof of the healing. Mark will often show Jesus upholding the Law's structure even as he transcends its function.

But the leper "went out, and began to proclaim it much, and to spread about the matter" (v. 45)—the opposite of the command. The consequence is ironic and revealing: "Jesus could no more openly enter into a city, but was outside in desert places. People came to him from everywhere." The more he tries to keep the secret, the more it spreads; the more the crowds press, the more he must withdraw. The leper's disobedience is, in its way, part of God's plan, for it drives Jesus toward the wilderness—the same wilderness of vv. 12–13—and it heightens the tension of the approaching conflict.

*Application:* The leper's story is a parable of the Christian approach to Christ: we come in humility, we confess our need ("you *can*"), and we trust his will ("if you *will*"). And it reminds us that God's purposes often work through our very imperfection—the very spreading we cannot control becomes the means by which more people meet the One who is holy and mightier than all.

---

#### Summary: The Theological Thread of the Chapter

Mark 1 is, in miniature, the whole Gospel. It moves from **promise** (the prophetic voice) to **anointing** (the baptism) to **proving** (the wilderness) to **proclamation** (the Kingdom kerygma) to **mission** (the calling, the preaching) and finally to **wonder** (the words and works that draw a crowd from "everywhere").

Three motifs run through the whole:

1. **The identity of Jesus** is declared (Son of God), confirmed (the wilderness), and confessed (by demon, by crowd)—yet deliberately held in the "secret" until the cross can fully reveal it.
2. **The Kingdom of God** is the subject of everything. It has drawn near; it demands repentance and faith; it is enacted in teaching, casting out, and healing.
3. **Authority** is the signature of the new age. He teaches, commands, heals, and calls—not by tradition, but as One who *can* and *wills*.

The chapter ends where a Gospel must end: with a growing crowd and a withdrawing Christ, heading toward the wilderness. The reader is left with the leper's question turned into faith—"If you want to, you can make me clean"—and with the answer that will echo to the end of the book: *I want to. Be made clean.*

**A closing prayer for the reader of the chapter:**

*Lord Jesus, Son of God, whose heavens were torn open at the Jordan and whose Spirit descended as a dove, teach me the posture of the leper—humble, dependent, trusting your will over my comfort. Give me the Spirit who drove you into the wilderness, that I may obey where Israel failed. And give me the discipline of the dark morning, that I rise to prayer before I rise to preaching, knowing that I came out for this reason: to proclaim the Good News that the Kingdom of God is at hand. In your name, amen.*

### Chapter 2

Chapter 2 of Mark's Gospel is one of the most Christologically dense passages in all of Scripture. In a single, brisk sequence, the evangelist gathers together four encounters that together reveal the identity of Jesus as Lord of heaven and earth: the Lord who forgives sin, who calls sinners, who inaugurates a new covenant, and who rules even over the Sabbath. The movement of the chapter is from authority claimed (the forgiveness of sins) to authority demonstrated (the healing, the calling, the redefinition of the sacred time). Let us go through it passage by passage.

---

#### I. The Paralytic and the Forgiveness of Sins (2:1–12)

**"When he entered again into Capernaum after some days, it was heard that he was at home."** The phrase *it was heard* carries weight. News of Jesus travels not by proclamation from pulpits but by the quiet, compelling movement of word of mouth. The house has become, in effect, a place of worship and healing. That *"there was no more room, not even around the door; and he spoke the word to them"* tells us the first thing of this chapter is this: **the Word of God still moves, even in a crowded, constrained place.** Jesus does not pause his teaching for the sake of the crowd; the crowd is made to receive what is already happening.

##### The faith of four

Four men come carrying a paralytic. What strikes the reader is the initiative of the friends, not of the sick man. The paralytic cannot even bring himself; he must be carried. And when the crowd blocks the way, they do not give up. *"They removed the roof where he was. When they had broken it up, they let down the mat that the paralytic was lying on."*

This is a picture of the Gospel itself. The man is helpless—paralyzed, as we are by sin—and his salvation comes through the determined, even sacrificial, effort of others who believe on his behalf. There is something here that prefigures the work of the church: we carry our struggling brothers, we break through the obstacles, we let them down before Christ. Mark notes, as he will throughout, that the decisive factor is not the miracle but the **faith** attached to it: *"Jesus, seeing their faith."* Notice he sees *their* faith—the men carrying him, and the paralytic lying in their care.

##### "Your sins are forgiven you"

And here the chapter turns, and the theological heart of Mark's Gospel is laid bare. Jesus does not first say, "Be healed." He says, *"Son, your sins are forgiven you."*

The use of *"Son"* is tender. In the original it is the intimate diminutive, *teknon*—child, little one. Before the man is a case for a miracle, he is a child for whom a debt stands. Jesus addresses the deepest wound before the most visible one. For the paralytic, and for all of us, the illness of the body is the shadow; the illness of the soul is the substance. We are all, in the last analysis, "paralytics" before God—unable to approach, unable to save ourselves, lying on a mat and waiting for someone to let us down before the throne of grace.

The scribes reason, *"Who can forgive sins but God alone?"* And they are right. This is the point. If only God can forgive sins, and Jesus stands in a room in Capernaum and forgives sins, then the only possible conclusion is the one that drives the Pharisees to blasphemy throughout the Gospels: **this man is God.** The charge of blasphemy they bring against Jesus is, paradoxically, the strongest confession of his divinity. He is accused of claiming what belongs to God alone; he answers the charge by doing exactly that and then performing a miracle as the visible seal.

##### The logic of the sign

*"Which is easier, to tell the paralytic, 'Your sins are forgiven,' or to say, 'Arise, and take up your bed, and walk'?"*

The question is deliberate and brilliant. To say "your sins are forgiven" is invisible, unverifiable, and—on the surface—easy to say, for it requires no physical power. To command a man to walk is, in human terms, both harder and demonstrable. Jesus' point: I do the harder, verifiable thing (the healing) precisely so that you may *know*—*"But that you may know that the Son of Man has authority on earth to forgive sins."* The bodily miracle is the **certificate** of the spiritual one. He makes the invisible visible. What he cannot yet let them see (the cleansing of the soul) he confirms by what they can (the walking of the body).

The title *"Son of Man"* is Mark's favorite, and here it is loaded with Daniel 7:13—the one coming on the clouds, to whom is given authority, glory, and an everlasting kingdom. The one whom the scribes think a blasphemer is the very one to whom the ancient vision gives the power to forgive.

And the man *"arose, and immediately took up the mat and went out in front of them all, so that they were all amazed and glorified God, saying, 'We never saw anything like this!'"* Note the object of their doxology: they glorify *God*, not Jesus. This is Mark's quiet, profound way of pointing to the Trinity. They see in the man of Nazareth the finger of God. The crowd's worship of God is itself a confession that they have just seen God at work in a human being.

---

#### II. The Calling of Levi the Tax Collector (2:13–17)

**"He went out again by the seaside. All the multitude came to him, and he taught them. As he passed by, he saw Levi the son of Alphaeus sitting at the tax office. He said to him, 'Follow me.' And he arose and followed him."**

The tempo of Mark is unmatched. *"He saw… he said… he arose and followed him."* No long negotiation, no letter of recommendation, no gradual preparation. The call is sudden, direct, and total. Levi leaves everything—the income, the position, the future he had planned—and follows.

Levi (Matthew, as we know from the other Gospels) is a **publican**, a tax collector working for the Roman occupation. In Jewish eyes he is a double outcast: a collaborator with the enemy, and a man who enriched himself, often by extortion, on his own people. That Jesus calls him is not a minor detail; it is the thesis of the whole chapter made flesh.

##### Eating with sinners

*"He was reclining at the table in his house, and many tax collectors and sinners sat down with Jesus and his disciples, for there were many, and they followed him."* The meal is the scene, because in the ancient world the table was the boundary marker of fellowship. To eat with a man was to declare him kin, worthy of one's company, cleansed in the sight of God. The Pharisees understand this, which is why they object: *"Why is it that he eats and drinks with tax collectors and sinners?"* They have made the table a place of exclusion; Jesus makes it a place of inclusion.

And Jesus' answer is one of the most famous and most comforting statements in the Gospels:

> *"Those who are healthy have no need for a physician, but those who are sick. I came not to call the righteous, but sinners to repentance."*

There is no one who reads this and can comfortably place himself among "the healthy." The claim of the religious is that they are well; the claim of Christ is that they are sick and in need of a doctor. This is the Gospel in its sharpest, most convicting, and most merciful form. Jesus does not come to admire the self-sufficient; he comes to the broken. And note the destination: he calls sinners *"to repentance"*—not to a new religion of self-approval, but to a turning back to God. The invitation is genuine, and its goal is holy. The table is not an end but a path.

This passage, more than almost any other, should shatter the self-righteousness of the comfortable church. If we are careful in our morals, if we avoid the obvious sins, we are not thereby "healthy" in Christ's judgment. We too are sick, and the physician is not here for the well but for us.

---

#### III. The Bridegroom, the New Wineskin (2:18–22)

**"John's disciples and the Pharisees were fasting, and they came and asked him, 'Why do John's disciples and the disciples of the Pharisees fast, but your disciples don't fast?'"**

Fasting was a mark of religious seriousness. The question, in the Pharisees' minds, is really: why is your movement so casual, so lacking in piety? Jesus answers not with a defense but with a declaration of joy:

> *"Can the groomsmen fast while the bridegroom is with them? As long as they have the bridegroom with them, they can't fast."*

The **bridegroom** image is one of the most theologically significant in the Gospels. It identifies Jesus not merely as the teacher or the prophet but as the divine bridegroom to whom the people of God are the bride. This is the language of Hosea and Isaiah and the Song of Songs made real. The reason the disciples do not fast is not that they are frivolous; it is that **the presence of the Messiah is a feast.** Fasting belongs to absence and mourning; the bridegroom is present, and the wedding is here. The Gospel is, at its core, wedding news.

But Jesus is honest about the future: *"the days will come when the bridegroom will be taken away from them, and then they will fast in that day."* Mark, writing to a community that has seen the cross and waits for the return, lets us hear the double echo: the taking away includes the passion, and the return brings the final wedding. The Christian life is lived in the tension between the presence and the absence of the bridegroom—rejoicing now, watching and waiting for the coming.

##### New wine in new wineskins

The two parables of the cloth and the wine are, at their root, one statement. An unshrunk patch on an old garment tears; new wine in old, rigid skins bursts the skins and wastes the wine. The point is not about textile technology. It is that **the new thing God is bringing in Christ cannot be contained within the old structures.** The old covenant, the old system of rules and boundaries, the old wineskin of religious form, is not broken by Jesus to be discarded in contempt—rather, it is outgrown. He is bringing a new creation, a new covenant, and it requires new vessels. To force the new life of the Spirit into the old skin of legalism produces either a torn garment or a burst skin and spilled wine.

For the church this is a standing warning. We are tempted, whenever a generation of innovation comes, to insist that the old wineskin must be preserved intact—and we burst the wine. Or we are tempted to tear out every seam and discard the whole garment—and we make a worse hole. The Spirit's work is the new wine; the task of the church is to hold it in vessels that are fresh enough to stretch and strong enough to keep.

---

#### IV. The Lord of the Sabbath (2:23–28)

**"He was going on the Sabbath day through the grain fields; and his disciples began, as they went, to pluck the ears of grain. The Pharisees said to him, 'Behold, why do they do that which is not lawful on the Sabbath day?'"**

The disciples are doing no more than the Mosaic law itself permits in principle: Deuteronomy 23:25 lets a man pluck ears in a neighbor's field with his hand, so long as he does not set a sickle to the standing grain. What the Pharisees have done, as they so often do in these pericopes, is to take a command of God and build around it a fence of human tradition so dense that plucking grain becomes "not lawful." They have made a **religion of the rules** in which the human need is subordinated to the regulation.

Jesus' answer reaches back to 1 Samuel 21: David, fleeing Saul and hungry, ate the showbread—the bread of the Presence in the tabernacle, which the law reserved for priests—under the hand of Abiathar the high priest. David did not commit an injustice; he committed a necessity. And the point Jesus draws is that **in the economy of God, mercy and human need outrank the rigid observance of the form.** The law was never meant to be a net that catches the hungry; it was meant to serve life.

And then the two great sentences that close the chapter:

> *"The Sabbath was made for man, not man for the Sabbath. Therefore the Son of Man is lord even of the Sabbath."*

The first is a restoration of the law's purpose. The Sabbath is a gift—a day of rest, of refreshment, of drawing near to God, of being released from the endless labor that marks fallen existence. It was given *for* the flourishing of man. When a rule begins to consume the person it was meant to serve, the rule has betrayed its origin. The Pharisees had inverted the order: the man was made to serve the Sabbath, bent under it, defined by it.
